<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that every finite simple undirected graph on <i>n</i> vertices has an edge partition into at most $`Cn`$ simple cycles and single edges, for an absolute constant <i>C</i>. This resolves the Erdős–Gallai cycle decomposition conjecture positively.

- **claim-main-linear-decomposition** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is an absolute constant $C>0$ such that, for every finite simple undirected graph $G$ with $n$ vertices, $E(G)$ has a partition into at most $Cn$ parts, each of which is the edge set of a cycle or consists of a single edge.
  - Explanation: Every finite simple undirected graph has an edge partition into linearly many parts, each a cycle or a single edge. The constant is absolute and does not depend on the graph.
- **claim-eulerian-cycle-partition** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles)): Every finite simple undirected Eulerian graph on $n$ vertices has an edge partition into at most $Cn$ cycles, with $C$ from [thm:main](#thm:main).* *For every fixed $\delta,p>0$, there exist $\varepsilon>0$ and an integer $n_0\ge1$ such that every finite simple undirected Eulerian graph $G$ on $n\ge n_0$ vertices has an edge partition into at most $\Delta(G)/2+\delta n$ cycles, provided that every partition $V(G)=A\mathbin{\dot\cup}B$ with $\|A\|,\|B\|\ge\varepsilon n$ satisfies $$e_G(A,B)\ge p\|A\|\|B\|.$$ Here $\Delta(G)$ denotes the maximum degree, and $e_G(A,B)$ is the number of edges joining $A$ to $B$.
  - Explanation: An Eulerian graph, one with all degrees even, can be partitioned into linearly many cycles with no single edges. A second assertion gives a bound near half the maximum degree under a large-cut condition.
- **claim-lovasz-path-cycle** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-lovasz)): The edges of a finite simple graph on $n$ vertices can be partitioned into at most $\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor$ paths and cycles.
  - Explanation: The edges of any finite simple graph split into at most half as many paths and cycles as there are vertices (rounded down).
- **claim-endpoint-bounded-paths** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-endpoint-paths)): Every finite simple graph on $n$ vertices has an edge partition into paths such that each vertex is an endpoint of at most two paths. In particular, the partition has at most $n$ paths.
  - Explanation: Every finite simple graph has an edge partition into paths in which each vertex is an endpoint of at most two paths, so there are at most n paths.
- **claim-aharoni-haxell-matching** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-aharoni-haxell)): Let $s$ be a positive integer and let $(\mathcal H_i)_{i\in J}$ be a finite indexed family of hypergraphs on a common ground set. Suppose every edge is nonempty and has size at most $s$. If, for every nonempty $I\subseteq J$, the union $\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal H_i$ has a matching of size strictly greater than $s(\|I\|-1)$, then there are edges $E_i\in\mathcal H_i$, one for each $i\in J$, that are pairwise disjoint.
  - Explanation: A Hall-type matching condition on unions of hypergraphs of bounded edge size gives pairwise disjoint representatives, one edge from each hypergraph.
- **claim-chernoff-binomial** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-chernoff)): If $X$ is binomial with mean $\mu$, then $$\Pr(X\le\mu/2)\le e^{-\mu/8}, \qquad \Pr(X\ge2\mu)\le e^{-\mu/3}.$$
  - Explanation: Exponential lower-tail and upper-tail bounds for a binomial variable around its mean.
- **claim-team-routing** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-routing)): Let $P$ be a graph of order $r\ge 2$ with cut expansion $h>0$, and put $$\label{eq:routing-radii} g=\left\lceil 8(r/h)\log(2r)\right\rceil,\qquad \ell=2g\bigl(1+\left\lceil\log r\right\rceil\bigr).$$ Let $\mathcal D$ be a finite set of demands, where demand $p$ has distinct endvertices $x_p,y_p\in V(P)$. Suppose that each vertex occurs as an endvertex in at most $k$ demands, where $k\ge 1$. Partition $\mathcal D$ into teams of size at most $b$, where $b\ge 1$, and give each demand a set $Z_p\subseteq V(P)$ of at most $z$ vertices, where $z\ge 0$. If $$\label{eq:routing-condition} h\ge 64\bigl(\ell^2(k+b)+z\bigr),$$ then there are simple $x_p$–$y_p$ paths $Q_p$ in $P$, one for each $p\in\mathcal D$, with the following properties:* 1. *each $Q_p$ has length at most $\ell$ and no internal vertex in $Z_p$;* 2. *the paths $Q_p$ are pairwise edge-disjoint;* 3. *the internal vertex sets of paths in the same team are pairwise disjoint.* *The sets $Z_p$ may contain $x_p$ or $y_p$: their restrictions concern only internal vertices. If a route must also avoid another demand’s endvertices internally, those vertices may be included in its set $Z_p$.
  - Explanation: In a graph with enough cut expansion, short routing paths exist for all demands. They are pairwise edge-disjoint, avoid prescribed forbidden vertices internally, and have internally disjoint paths within each team.
- **claim-edge-splitting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-edge-split)): Fix $c>0$ and a positive integer $l$. For all sufficiently large $D$, if an $r$-vertex graph $P$ has cut expansion at least $h_0\ge cD^{0.90}$ and $D^{0.90}\le r\le D^{1.02}$, then its edge set can be partitioned into $l$ spanning subgraphs, each with cut expansion at least $h_0/(2l)$.
  - Explanation: An expanding graph at scale D can be split into a fixed number of spanning subgraphs, each keeping a proportional fraction of the cut expansion.
- **claim-vertex-sampling** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-vertex-sampling)): Fix $c'>0$ and $p\in(0,1)$. Let $P$ have order $D^{0.90}\le r\le D^{1.02}$ and cut expansion at least $h=c'D^{0.90}$. Select each vertex independently with probability $p$ to form $V'$. With probability $1-o(1)$ as $D\to\infty$, all of the following hold: $$\label{eq:residue-sampling-properties} \begin{gathered} \|V'\|\le2pr,\qquad \|N_P(v)\cap V'\|\ge ph/2\quad(v\in V(P)),\\ P[V']\text{ has cut expansion at least }\tau=D^{0.74}. \end{gathered}$$ The error term is uniform over the stated graphs and orders, with $c'$ and $p$ fixed.
  - Explanation: A random vertex sample of an expanding graph is, with probability tending to one, of controlled size, has many sampled neighbours at every vertex, and induces a subgraph with polynomial cut expansion.
- **claim-small-residue** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-small-residue)): Fix $\eta>0$ and $c>0$. For all sufficiently large $D$, let $G$ be an $r$-vertex graph with $D^{0.90}\le r\le D^{1.02}$ containing a spanning subgraph of cut expansion at least $cD^{0.90}$. Then $E(G)$ has a partition into at most $9r$ completed parts, each a simple cycle or a single edge, and the edge sets of at most three residual simple graphs. The residual graphs have pairwise disjoint vertex sets contained in $V(G)$, their total order is at most $\eta r$, and each has order strictly less than $r$.
  - Explanation: A graph containing a spanning expander has its edges partitioned into linearly many completed cycles and single edges plus at most three residual graphs of arbitrarily small total order.
- **claim-scale-splitting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-item-scale-expanders)): There are absolute constants $D_{\mathrm{sp}}$ and $C_0$ such that, for every real $D\ge D_{\mathrm{sp}}$, every graph $G$ on a nonempty vertex set of order $N$ and with average degree at most $D$ admits the following two successive operations. One may take $C_0=2$.* 1. * Remove a family $\mathcal C$ of edge-disjoint cycles, each of length at least $D^{1.01}$, where $$\label{eq:scale-cycle-cost} \left\|\mathcal C\right\|\le \frac{N}{2D^{0.01}}.$$ Partition the remaining edges into graphs $G_Y$ on an indexed family $\mathcal Y$ of vertex sets, called boxes. These satisfy $$\label{eq:scale-box-order} \begin{gathered} \bigcup_{Y\in\mathcal Y}Y=V(G),\qquad 1\le \left\|Y\right\|\le D^{1.02},\\ \sum_{Y\in\mathcal Y}\left\|Y\right\| \le \left(1+\frac{C_0}{\log D}\right)N. \end{gathered}$$ The graphs $G_Y$ contain only their assigned edges; their vertex sets may overlap.* 2. *Put $\sigma=D^{0.90}$. For each box $Y$, partition $E(G_Y)$ into the edge sets of a family $\mathcal R_Y$ of graphs $R$ and a graph $H_Y$. The vertex sets of the graphs in $\mathcal R_Y$ are pairwise disjoint subsets of $Y$. Each $R$ has cut expansion at least $\sigma$ and order $$\label{eq:scale-piece-order} \sigma\le \left\|V(R)\right\|\le D^{1.02}.$$ The residual graph $H_Y$ is taken on all of $Y$, including isolated vertices, and satisfies $$\label{eq:scale-residual-degree} \frac{2\left\|E(H_Y)\right\|}{\left\|Y\right\|}\le D^{0.95}.$$* *Thus the cycles in $\mathcal C$, the pieces $R$, and the residual graphs $H_Y$ together partition $E(G)$.
  - Explanation: At each scale, long cycles are removed, the rest is assigned to overlapping boxes of controlled total order, and expanding pieces are extracted in each box leaving a sparse residual graph.
- **claim-vertex-expansion-long-cycle** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-split-vertex-expansion)): Fix $\varepsilon=1/32$. For every sufficiently large integer $u$, put $\alpha=\varepsilon/\log^2 u$. If a graph $H$ of order $u$ satisfies $$\label{eq:split-vertex-expansion} \left\|N_H(U)\setminus U\right\|\ge\alpha\left\|U\right\| \qquad\left(0<\left\|U\right\|\le\frac u2\right),$$ then $H$ contains a simple cycle of length greater than $$\label{eq:split-long-cycle-length} q(u)=\frac{\alpha^2u}{100} =\frac{\varepsilon^2u}{100\log^4u}.$$
  - Explanation: Sufficient vertex expansion of all small sets forces a long simple cycle, proved by a depth-first search balance argument.
- **claim-pair-folding** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-pair-folding)): Let $J$ be a finite simple graph and let $X_1,\ldots,X_t$ be pairwise disjoint subsets of its vertex set. Suppose that $a\ge1$, that $m_i=\left\|X_i\right\|\ge\max\{8,a^2\}$ for every $i$, and that $$\begin{aligned} d_J(x)&\le a &&\text{for every }x\in\bigcup_i X_i,\label{eq:fold-degree}\\ d_i(v):=\left\|N_J(v)\cap X_i\right\|&\le a &&\text{for every }v\in V(J)\text{ and every }i. \label{eq:fold-neighbors} \end{aligned}$$ There are pairings within the $X_i$, each leaving exactly $m_i\bmod2$ vertices unpaired, and a set $E_{\rm err}\subseteq E(J)$ with $$\left\|E_{\rm err}\right\|\le 2\sum_i m_i,$$ such that identifying each pair in $J-E_{\rm err}$ gives a simple graph $Q$. Every edge of $Q$ has exactly one retained original edge as its representative. Retaining all quotient vertices, including isolated ones, we have $$\label{eq:fold-order} \left\|V(Q)\right\|=\left\|V(J)\right\|-\sum_i\left\lfloor m_i/2\right\rfloor.$$ The assertion also holds when the family of folding sets is empty.
  - Explanation: Pairing vertices inside disjoint sets under bounded degree conditions gives a simple quotient graph after setting aside a linear number of error edges.
- **claim-batch-resolution** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-batch-resolution)): For every sufficiently large real $D$, set $$a=D^{0.01},\qquad L_0=D^{0.03},\qquad B_0=D^{0.30},\qquad \sigma=D^{0.90}.$$ Let $Y$ be a finite vertex set. Let $\mathcal W$ be a finite indexed family of subsets $W\subseteq Y$, with $\left\|W\right\|\le B_0$, and let $J_W$ be a simple graph on $W$ for each index. Suppose these graphs have pairwise disjoint edge sets, and let $F=F(Y)$ be their union, taken on $Y$.* *Let $\mathcal R$ be a family of simple graphs on pairwise disjoint vertex subsets of $Y$. Suppose that each $R\in\mathcal R$ has order $\sigma\le r_R\le D^{1.02}$, that $E(R)\cap E(F)=\varnothing$, and that its edges are partitioned into two spanning graphs $P_R,T_R$, both of cut expansion at least $\sigma/4$. Call $P_R$ the router.* *For some pairs $(W,R)$ let an active set $X_{W,R}\subseteq W\cap V(R)$ be specified, satisfying $$\label{eq:batch-active-hypotheses} \left\|X_{W,R}\right\|\ge L_0, \qquad d_F(x)\le a\quad(x\in X_{W,R}).$$ Sums over $(W,R)$ below include only active sets, and write $\Sigma_Y=\sum_{W,R}\left\|X_{W,R}\right\|$.* *There are finite simple quotient graphs $Q_W$, with all quotient vertices retained, whose orders satisfy $$\begin{aligned} q_W&=\left\|W\right\|-\sum_{R:\,(W,R)\text{ active}} \left\lfloor\left\|X_{W,R}\right\|/2\right\rfloor\notag\\ &\le\left\|W\right\|-\frac13 \sum_{R:\,(W,R)\text{ active}}\left\|X_{W,R}\right\| \le B_0,\label{eq:batch-quotient-order}\\ \sum_W q_W&\le\sum_W\left\|W\right\|-\frac13\Sigma_Y. \label{eq:batch-total-order} \end{aligned}$$ For every choice of cycle-and-edge partitions of these $Q_W$, having $t_W$ parts, there are sets $U_R\subseteq E(P_R)$ and a cycle-and-edge partition of $$E(F)\mathbin{\dot\cup}\mathop{\dot\bigcup}_{R\in\mathcal R}U_R$$ with at most $$\label{eq:batch-parts} \sum_W t_W+8\Sigma_Y$$ parts. The graphs $R'=R-U_R$, kept on $V(R)$, contain the untouched spanning graphs $T_R$, so each still has a spanning subgraph of cut expansion at least $\sigma/4$. The completed parts and these remaining graphs together partition $E(F)\mathbin{\dot\cup}\mathop{\dot\bigcup}_{R\in\mathcal R}E(R)$. All thresholds in this lemma are absolute.
  - Explanation: Quotient graphs of batches lose order, and any cycle-and-edge partitions of them lift to the original edges using routers in expanding pieces, at a bounded extra cost while keeping an untouched expanding spanning subgraph in each piece.
- **claim-prefix-selection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-prefix-choice)): Let $(S_j)_{j\ge0}$ be a nonnegative, finitely supported sequence which is not identically zero. There is an index $i$ with $S_i>0$ such that $$\label{eq:prefix-charge} \sum_{j=0}^{i+K}S_j\le P_0S_i.$$
  - Explanation: For any nonzero finitely supported nonnegative sequence there is a positive term whose prefix sum up to a fixed window beyond it is bounded by a constant multiple of that term.
- **claim-induction-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-induction-bound)): There is an absolute constant $C$ such that every finite simple graph on $n\ge1$ vertices has an edge partition into at most $C\phi(n)n$ simple cycles and single edges.
  - Explanation: A strengthened inductive bound with a slowly decreasing potential factor, from which the main linear theorem follows.

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-vertex-sampling",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $c'>0$ and $p\\in(0,1)$. Let $P$ have order $D^{0.90}\\le r\\le D^{1.02}$ and cut expansion at least $h=c'D^{0.90}$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-small-residue",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $\\eta>0$ and $c>0$. For all sufficiently large $D$, let $G$ be an $r$-vertex graph with $D^{0.90}\\le r\\le D^{1.02}$ containing a spanning subgraph of cut expansion at least $cD^{0.90}$. Then $E(G)$ has a partition into at most $9r$ completed parts, each a simple cycle or a single edge, and the edge sets of at most three residual simple graphs.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-item-scale-expanders",
        "exact_quote": "<span id=\"lem:scale-split\" label=\"lem:scale-split\"></span> There are absolute constants $D_{\\mathrm{sp}}$ and $C_0$ such that, for every real $D\\ge D_{\\mathrm{sp}}$, every graph $G$ on a nonempty vertex set of order $N$ and with average degree at most $D$ admits the following two successive operations. One may take $C_0=2$.*\n\n\n\n1.  *<span id=\"item:scale-boxes\" label=\"item:scale-boxes\"></span> Remove a family $\\mathcal C$ of edge-disjoint cycles, each of length at least $D^{1.01}$, where $$\\label{eq:scale-cycle-cost}\n      \\left|\\mathcal C\\right|\\le \\frac{N}{2D^{0.01}}.$$ Partition the remaining edges into graphs $G_Y$ on an indexed family $\\mathcal Y$ of vertex sets, called boxes. These satisfy $$\\label{eq:scale-box-order}\n    \\begin{gathered}\n      \\bigcup_{Y\\in\\mathcal Y}Y=V(G),\\qquad\n      1\\le \\left|Y\\right|\\le D^{1.02},\\\\\n      \\sum_{Y\\in\\mathcal Y}\\left|Y\\right|\n        \\le \\left(1+\\frac{C_0}{\\log D}\\right)N.\n    \\end{gathered}$$ The graphs $G_Y$ contain only their assigned edges; their vertex sets may overlap.*\n\n\n2.  *Put $\\sigma=D^{0.90}$. For each box $Y$, partition $E(G_Y)$ into the edge sets of a family $\\mathcal R_Y$ of graphs $R$ and a graph $H_Y$. The vertex sets of the graphs in $\\mathcal R_Y$ are pairwise disjoint subsets of $Y$. Each $R$ has cut expansion at least $\\sigma$ and order $$\\label{eq:scale-piece-order}\n      \\sigma\\le \\left|V(R)\\right|\\le D^{1.02}.$$ The residual graph $H_Y$ is taken on all of $Y$, including isolated vertices, and satisfies $$\\label{eq:scale-residual-degree}\n      \\frac{2\\left|E(H_Y)\\right|}{\\left|Y\\right|}\\le D^{0.95}.$$*\n\n*Thus the cycles in $\\mathcal C$, the pieces $R$, and the residual graphs $H_Y$ together partition $E(G)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-181.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-item-scale-expanders",
          "exact_quote": "<span id=\"lem:scale-split\" label=\"lem:scale-split\"></span> There are absolute constants $D_{\\mathrm{sp}}$ and $C_0$ such that, for every real $D\\ge D_{\\mathrm{sp}}$, every graph $G$ on a nonempty vertex set of order $N$ and with average degree at most $D$ admits the following two successive operations. These satisfy $$\\label{eq:scale-box-order}\n    \\begin{gathered}\n      \\bigcup_{Y\\in\\mathcal Y}Y=V(G),\\qquad\n      1\\le \\left|Y\\right|\\le D^{1.02},\\\\\n      \\sum_{Y\\in\\mathcal Y}\\left|Y\\right|\n        \\le \\left(1+\\frac{C_0}{\\log D}\\right)N.\n    \\end{gathered}$$ The graphs $G_Y$ contain only their assigned edges; their vertex sets may overlap.*\n\n\n2.  *Put $\\sigma=D^{0.90}$. For each box $Y$, partition $E(G_Y)$ into the edge sets of a family $\\mathcal R_Y$ of graphs $R$ and a graph $H_Y$. The vertex sets of the graphs in $\\mathcal R_Y$ are pairwise disjoint subsets of $Y$. Each $R$ has cut expansion at least $\\sigma$ and order $$\\label{eq:scale-piece-order}\n      \\sigma\\le \\left|V(R)\\right|\\le D^{1.02}.$$ The residual graph $H_Y$ is taken on all of $Y$, including isolated vertices, and satisfies $$\\label{eq:scale-residual-degree}\n      \\frac{2\\left|E(H_Y)\\right|}{\\left|Y\\right|}\\le D^{0.95}.$$*\n\n*Thus the cycles in $\\mathcal C$, the pieces $R$, and the residual graphs $H_Y$ together partition $E(G)$.",
          "source_id": "PM-181.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "<span id=\"lem:scale-split\" label=\"lem:scale-split\"></span> There are absolute constants $D_{\\mathrm{sp}}$ and $C_0$ such that, for every real $D\\ge D_{\\mathrm{sp}}$, every graph $G$ on a nonempty vertex set of order $N$ and with average degree at most $D$ admits the following two successive operations. One may take $C_0=2$.*\n\n\n\n1.  *<span id=\"item:scale-boxes\" label=\"item:scale-boxes\"></span> Remove a family $\\mathcal C$ of edge-disjoint cycles, each of length at least $D^{1.01}$, where $$\\label{eq:scale-cycle-cost}\n      \\left|\\mathcal C\\right|\\le \\frac{N}{2D^{0.01}}.$$ Partition the remaining edges into graphs $G_Y$ on an indexed family $\\mathcal Y$ of vertex sets, called boxes. These satisfy $$\\label{eq:scale-box-order}\n    \\begin{gathered}\n      \\bigcup_{Y\\in\\mathcal Y}Y=V(G),\\qquad\n      1\\le \\left|Y\\right|\\le D^{1.02},\\\\\n      \\sum_{Y\\in\\mathcal Y}\\left|Y\\right|\n        \\le \\left(1+\\frac{C_0}{\\log D}\\right)N.\n    \\end{gathered}$$ The graphs $G_Y$ contain only their assigned edges; their vertex sets may overlap.*\n\n\n2.  *Put $\\sigma=D^{0.90}$. For each box $Y$, partition $E(G_Y)$ into the edge sets of a family $\\mathcal R_Y$ of graphs $R$ and a graph $H_Y$. Each $R$ has cut expansion at least $\\sigma$ and order $$\\label{eq:scale-piece-order}\n      \\sigma\\le \\left|V(R)\\right|\\le D^{1.02}.$$ The residual graph $H_Y$ is taken on all of $Y$, including isolated vertices, and satisfies $$\\label{eq:scale-residual-degree}\n      \\frac{2\\left|E(H_Y)\\right|}{\\left|Y\\right|}\\le D^{0.95}.$$*\n\n*Thus the cycles in $\\mathcal C$, the pieces $R$, and the residual graphs $H_Y$ together partition $E(G)$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-split-vertex-expansion",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $\\varepsilon=1/32$. For every sufficiently large integer $u$, put $\\alpha=\\varepsilon/\\log^2 u$. If a graph $H$ of order $u$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:split-vertex-expansion}\n  \\left|N_H(U)\\setminus U\\right|\\ge\\alpha\\left|U\\right|\n  \\qquad\\left(0<\\left|U\\right|\\le\\frac u2\\right),$$ then $H$ contains a simple cycle of length greater than $$\\label{eq:split-long-cycle-length}\n  q(u)=\\frac{\\alpha^2u}{100}\n      =\\frac{\\varepsilon^2u}{100\\log^4u}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-181.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pair-folding",
        "exact_quote": "Let $J$ be a finite simple graph and let $X_1,\\ldots,X_t$ be pairwise disjoint subsets of its vertex set. Suppose that $a\\ge1$, that $m_i=\\left|X_i\\right|\\ge\\max\\{8,a^2\\}$ for every $i$, and that $$\\begin{aligned}\n d_J(x)&\\le a &&\\text{for every }x\\in\\bigcup_i X_i,\\label{eq:fold-degree}\\\\\n d_i(v):=\\left|N_J(v)\\cap X_i\\right|&\\le a\n     &&\\text{for every }v\\in V(J)\\text{ and every }i.\n     \\label{eq:fold-neighbors}\n\\end{aligned}$$ There are pairings within the $X_i$, each leaving exactly $m_i\\bmod2$ vertices unpaired, and a set $E_{\\rm err}\\subseteq E(J)$ with $$\\left|E_{\\rm err}\\right|\\le 2\\sum_i m_i,$$ such that identifying each pair in $J-E_{\\rm err}$ gives a simple graph $Q$. Every edge of $Q$ has exactly one retained original edge as its representative. Retaining all quotient vertices, including isolated ones, we have $$\\label{eq:fold-order}\n \\left|V(Q)\\right|=\\left|V(J)\\right|-\\sum_i\\left\\lfloor m_i/2\\right\\rfloor.$$ The assertion also holds when the family of folding sets is empty.",
        "source_id": "PM-181.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pair-folding",
          "exact_quote": "Let $J$ be a finite simple graph and let $X_1,\\ldots,X_t$ be pairwise disjoint subsets of its vertex set. Suppose that $a\\ge1$, that $m_i=\\left|X_i\\right|\\ge\\max\\{8,a^2\\}$ for every $i$, and that $$\\begin{aligned}\n d_J(x)&\\le a &&\\text{for every }x\\in\\bigcup_i X_i,\\label{eq:fold-degree}\\\\\n d_i(v):=\\left|N_J(v)\\cap X_i\\right|&\\le a\n     &&\\text{for every }v\\in V(J)\\text{ and every }i.\n     \\label{eq:fold-neighbors}\n\\end{aligned}$$ There are pairings within the $X_i$, each leaving exactly $m_i\\bmod2$ vertices unpaired, and a set $E_{\\rm err}\\subseteq E(J)$ with $$\\left|E_{\\rm err}\\right|\\le 2\\sum_i m_i,$$ such that identifying each pair in $J-E_{\\rm err}$ gives a simple graph $Q$. Every edge of $Q$ has exactly one retained original edge as its representative. Retaining all quotient vertices, including isolated ones, we have $$\\label{eq:fold-order}\n \\left|V(Q)\\right|=\\left|V(J)\\right|-\\sum_i\\left\\lfloor m_i/2\\right\\rfloor.$$ The assertion also holds when the family of folding sets is empty.",
          "source_id": "PM-181.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $J$ be a finite simple graph and let $X_1,\\ldots,X_t$ be pairwise disjoint subsets of its vertex set. Suppose that $a\\ge1$, that $m_i=\\left|X_i\\right|\\ge\\max\\{8,a^2\\}$ for every $i$, and that $$\\begin{aligned}\n d_J(x)&\\le a &&\\text{for every }x\\in\\bigcup_i X_i,\\label{eq:fold-degree}\\\\\n d_i(v):=\\left|N_J(v)\\cap X_i\\right|&\\le a\n     &&\\text{for every }v\\in V(J)\\text{ and every }i.\n     \\label{eq:fold-neighbors}\n\\end{aligned}$$ There are pairings within the $X_i$, each leaving exactly $m_i\\bmod2$ vertices unpaired, and a set $E_{\\rm err}\\subseteq E(J)$ with $$\\left|E_{\\rm err}\\right|\\le 2\\sum_i m_i,$$ such that identifying each pair in $J-E_{\\rm err}$ gives a simple graph $Q$. Every edge of $Q$ has exactly one retained original edge as its representative. Retaining all quotient vertices, including isolated ones, we have $$\\label{eq:fold-order}\n \\left|V(Q)\\right|=\\left|V(J)\\right|-\\sum_i\\left\\lfloor m_i/2\\right\\rfloor.$$ The assertion also holds when the family of folding sets is empty.",
      "explanation": "Pairing vertices inside disjoint sets under bounded degree conditions gives a simple quotient graph after setting aside a linear number of error edges.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pair-folding",

      "id": "claim-pair-folding",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-batch-resolution",
        "exact_quote": "For every sufficiently large real $D$, set $$a=D^{0.01},\\qquad L_0=D^{0.03},\\qquad\n B_0=D^{0.30},\\qquad \\sigma=D^{0.90}.$$ Let $Y$ be a finite vertex set. Let $\\mathcal W$ be a finite indexed family of subsets $W\\subseteq Y$, with $\\left|W\\right|\\le B_0$, and let $J_W$ be a simple graph on $W$ for each index. Suppose these graphs have pairwise disjoint edge sets, and let $F=F(Y)$ be their union, taken on $Y$.*\n\n*Let $\\mathcal R$ be a family of simple graphs on pairwise disjoint vertex subsets of $Y$. Suppose that each $R\\in\\mathcal R$ has order $\\sigma\\le r_R\\le D^{1.02}$, that $E(R)\\cap E(F)=\\varnothing$, and that its edges are partitioned into two spanning graphs $P_R,T_R$, both of cut expansion at least $\\sigma/4$. Call $P_R$ the router.*\n\n*For some pairs $(W,R)$ let an active set $X_{W,R}\\subseteq W\\cap V(R)$ be specified, satisfying $$\\label{eq:batch-active-hypotheses}\n \\left|X_{W,R}\\right|\\ge L_0,\n \\qquad d_F(x)\\le a\\quad(x\\in X_{W,R}).$$ Sums over $(W,R)$ below include only active sets, and write $\\Sigma_Y=\\sum_{W,R}\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|$.*\n\n\n\n\n*There are finite simple quotient graphs $Q_W$, with all quotient vertices retained, whose orders satisfy $$\\begin{aligned}\n q_W&=\\left|W\\right|-\\sum_{R:\\,(W,R)\\text{ active}}\n                 \\left\\lfloor\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|/2\\right\\rfloor\\notag\\\\\n &\\le\\left|W\\right|-\\frac13\n            \\sum_{R:\\,(W,R)\\text{ active}}\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|\n \\le B_0,\\label{eq:batch-quotient-order}\\\\\n \\sum_W q_W&\\le\\sum_W\\left|W\\right|-\\frac13\\Sigma_Y.\n \\label{eq:batch-total-order}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For every choice of cycle-and-edge partitions of these $Q_W$, having $t_W$ parts, there are sets $U_R\\subseteq E(P_R)$ and a cycle-and-edge partition of $$E(F)\\mathbin{\\dot\\cup}\\mathop{\\dot\\bigcup}_{R\\in\\mathcal R}U_R$$ with at most $$\\label{eq:batch-parts}\n \\sum_W t_W+8\\Sigma_Y$$ parts. The graphs $R'=R-U_R$, kept on $V(R)$, contain the untouched spanning graphs $T_R$, so each still has a spanning subgraph of cut expansion at least $\\sigma/4$. The completed parts and these remaining graphs together partition $E(F)\\mathbin{\\dot\\cup}\\mathop{\\dot\\bigcup}_{R\\in\\mathcal R}E(R)$. All thresholds in this lemma are absolute.",
        "source_id": "PM-181.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-batch-resolution",
          "exact_quote": "For every sufficiently large real $D$, set $$a=D^{0.01},\\qquad L_0=D^{0.03},\\qquad\n B_0=D^{0.30},\\qquad \\sigma=D^{0.90}.$$ Let $Y$ be a finite vertex set. Let $\\mathcal W$ be a finite indexed family of subsets $W\\subseteq Y$, with $\\left|W\\right|\\le B_0$, and let $J_W$ be a simple graph on $W$ for each index. Suppose these graphs have pairwise disjoint edge sets, and let $F=F(Y)$ be their union, taken on $Y$.*\n\n*Let $\\mathcal R$ be a family of simple graphs on pairwise disjoint vertex subsets of $Y$. Suppose that each $R\\in\\mathcal R$ has order $\\sigma\\le r_R\\le D^{1.02}$, that $E(R)\\cap E(F)=\\varnothing$, and that its edges are partitioned into two spanning graphs $P_R,T_R$, both of cut expansion at least $\\sigma/4$. Call $P_R$ the router.*\n\n*For some pairs $(W,R)$ let an active set $X_{W,R}\\subseteq W\\cap V(R)$ be specified, satisfying $$\\label{eq:batch-active-hypotheses}\n \\left|X_{W,R}\\right|\\ge L_0,\n \\qquad d_F(x)\\le a\\quad(x\\in X_{W,R}).$$ Sums over $(W,R)$ below include only active sets, and write $\\Sigma_Y=\\sum_{W,R}\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|$.*\n\n\n\n\n*There are finite simple quotient graphs $Q_W$, with all quotient vertices retained, whose orders satisfy $$\\begin{aligned}\n q_W&=\\left|W\\right|-\\sum_{R:\\,(W,R)\\text{ active}}\n                 \\left\\lfloor\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|/2\\right\\rfloor\\notag\\\\\n &\\le\\left|W\\right|-\\frac13\n            \\sum_{R:\\,(W,R)\\text{ active}}\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|\n \\le B_0,\\label{eq:batch-quotient-order}\\\\\n \\sum_W q_W&\\le\\sum_W\\left|W\\right|-\\frac13\\Sigma_Y.\n \\label{eq:batch-total-order}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For every choice of cycle-and-edge partitions of these $Q_W$, having $t_W$ parts, there are sets $U_R\\subseteq E(P_R)$ and a cycle-and-edge partition of $$E(F)\\mathbin{\\dot\\cup}\\mathop{\\dot\\bigcup}_{R\\in\\mathcal R}U_R$$ with at most $$\\label{eq:batch-parts}\n \\sum_W t_W+8\\Sigma_Y$$ parts. The graphs $R'=R-U_R$, kept on $V(R)$, contain the untouched spanning graphs $T_R$, so each still has a spanning subgraph of cut expansion at least $\\sigma/4$. The completed parts and these remaining graphs together partition $E(F)\\mathbin{\\dot\\cup}\\mathop{\\dot\\bigcup}_{R\\in\\mathcal R}E(R)$. All thresholds in this lemma are absolute.",
          "source_id": "PM-181.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every sufficiently large real $D$, set $$a=D^{0.01},\\qquad L_0=D^{0.03},\\qquad\n B_0=D^{0.30},\\qquad \\sigma=D^{0.90}.$$ Let $Y$ be a finite vertex set. Let $\\mathcal W$ be a finite indexed family of subsets $W\\subseteq Y$, with $\\left|W\\right|\\le B_0$, and let $J_W$ be a simple graph on $W$ for each index. Suppose these graphs have pairwise disjoint edge sets, and let $F=F(Y)$ be their union, taken on $Y$.*\n\n*Let $\\mathcal R$ be a family of simple graphs on pairwise disjoint vertex subsets of $Y$. Suppose that each $R\\in\\mathcal R$ has order $\\sigma\\le r_R\\le D^{1.02}$, that $E(R)\\cap E(F)=\\varnothing$, and that its edges are partitioned into two spanning graphs $P_R,T_R$, both of cut expansion at least $\\sigma/4$. Call $P_R$ the router.*\n\n*For some pairs $(W,R)$ let an active set $X_{W,R}\\subseteq W\\cap V(R)$ be specified, satisfying $$\\label{eq:batch-active-hypotheses}\n \\left|X_{W,R}\\right|\\ge L_0,\n \\qquad d_F(x)\\le a\\quad(x\\in X_{W,R}).$$ Sums over $(W,R)$ below include only active sets, and write $\\Sigma_Y=\\sum_{W,R}\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|$.*\n\n\n\n\n*There are finite simple quotient graphs $Q_W$, with all quotient vertices retained, whose orders satisfy $$\\begin{aligned}\n q_W&=\\left|W\\right|-\\sum_{R:\\,(W,R)\\text{ active}}\n                 \\left\\lfloor\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|/2\\right\\rfloor\\notag\\\\\n &\\le\\left|W\\right|-\\frac13\n            \\sum_{R:\\,(W,R)\\text{ active}}\\left|X_{W,R}\\right|\n \\le B_0,\\label{eq:batch-quotient-order}\\\\\n \\sum_W q_W&\\le\\sum_W\\left|W\\right|-\\frac13\\Sigma_Y.\n \\label{eq:batch-total-order}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For every choice of cycle-and-edge partitions of these $Q_W$, having $t_W$ parts, there are sets $U_R\\subseteq E(P_R)$ and a cycle-and-edge partition of $$E(F)\\mathbin{\\dot\\cup}\\mathop{\\dot\\bigcup}_{R\\in\\mathcal R}U_R$$ with at most $$\\label{eq:batch-parts}\n \\sum_W t_W+8\\Sigma_Y$$ parts. The graphs $R'=R-U_R$, kept on $V(R)$, contain the untouched spanning graphs $T_R$, so each still has a spanning subgraph of cut expansion at least $\\sigma/4$. The completed parts and these remaining graphs together partition $E(F)\\mathbin{\\dot\\cup}\\mathop{\\dot\\bigcup}_{R\\in\\mathcal R}E(R)$. All thresholds in this lemma are absolute.",
      "explanation": "Quotient graphs of batches lose order, and any cycle-and-edge partitions of them lift to the original edges using routers in expanding pieces, at a bounded extra cost while keeping an untouched expanding spanning subgraph in each piece.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **cycle** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Here and throughout, a cycle is simple and has length at least three.
- **Eulerian graph** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Here a graph is *Eulerian* if all its degrees are even; connectivity is not required.
- **path** ([source](#pm-h-5)): A path is simple and has at least one edge.
- **order of a graph** ([source](#pm-h-5)): The *order* of a graph is its number of vertices, including any isolated vertices that have been retained.
- **partition by an indexed family of subgraphs** ([source](#pm-h-5)): An indexed family of subgraphs partitions a graph when their assigned edge sets are pairwise disjoint and have the required union.
- **edge set between vertex sets** ([source](#pm-h-5)): For disjoint vertex sets $A,B$, write $E_H(A,B)$ for the set of edges of $H$ between them and $e_H(A,B)=\|E_H(A,B)\|$.
- **edge boundary** ([source](#pm-h-5)): Write $\partial_H U$ for the edge boundary of $U\subseteq V(H)$.
- **logarithm convention** ([source](#pm-h-5)): All logarithms denoted by $\log$ are to base two; $\ln$ denotes the natural logarithm.
- **hypergraph** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A hypergraph here is a finite family of nonempty subsets of a finite ground set.
- **matching** ([source](#pm-h-7)): A matching is a subfamily of pairwise disjoint edges.
- **demand** ([source](#sec:routing)): A demand specifies two distinct vertices to be joined.
- **external neighbor set** ([source](#sec:splitting)): For a vertex set $U$ in a graph $H$, write $N_H(U)=\bigcup_{v\in U}N_H(v)$; its external neighbor set is $N_H(U)\setminus U$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-lovasz-input** [claim](#stmt-thm-lovasz) / [proof](#pm-h-6): Cited external decomposition theorem -> Path-and-cycle partition bound
- **step-endpoint-paths** [claim](#stmt-cor-endpoint-paths) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles): Add an auxiliary vertex to make degrees odd, then apply the path-cycle theorem and delete the vertex -> Path partition with endpoint load at most two
- **step-hypergraph-matching** [claim](#stmt-thm-aharoni-haxell) / [proof](#pm-h-7): Cited external hypergraph Hall theorem -> Disjoint representatives
- **step-chernoff** [claim](#stmt-lem-chernoff) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-chernoff): Exponential moment and Markov inequality -> Binomial tail bounds
- **step-routing** [claim](#stmt-lem-routing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-routing): Reduce to hypergraph matching and verify the Hall condition by ball growth in expanders -> Edge-disjoint routes with team constraints
- **step-edge-split** [claim](#stmt-lem-edge-split) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-edge-split): Random assignment of edges to classes, Chernoff bound and union bound over cuts -> Spanning subgraphs with proportional expansion
- **step-vertex-sampling** [claim](#stmt-lem-vertex-sampling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-vertex-sampling): Chernoff bounds plus a witness family of neighbourhood unions approximating failing cuts -> Sampled reservoir with expansion
- **step-small-residue** [claim](#stmt-lem-small-residue) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-small-residue): Three reservoirs, path partitions, routing to close paths into simple cycles -> Completed parts and small residual graphs
- **step-long-cycle** [claim](#stmt-lem-split-vertex-expansion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-split-vertex-expansion): Depth-first search balance point and shortest bypass path -> Long cycle from vertex expansion
- **step-scale-split** [claim](#stmt-item-scale-expanders) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-split-vertex-expansion-2): Remove long cycles, overlapping splits with a potential function, small-side cut extraction -> Boxes, expanding pieces and sparse residuals
- **step-pair-folding** [claim](#stmt-lem-pair-folding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pair-folding): Random pairings and expectation of loop and collision errors -> Simple quotient graph
- **step-batch-resolution** [claim](#stmt-lem-batch-resolution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-batch-resolution): Trim heavy neighbours, fold, lift quotient cycles through routed switch paths -> Lifted cycle-and-edge partition with preserved expander
- **step-prefix-choice** [claim](#stmt-lem-prefix-choice) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prefix-choice): Moving-index argument maintaining an invariant -> Index with bounded prefix sum
- **step-induction** [claim](#stmt-prop-induction-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-induction-bound): Strong induction using scale layers, prefix choice, folding, batch lifting and small residues -> Bound with potential factor
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-47): Specialise the inductive bound using phi at most one -> Linear bound for all graphs
- **step-eulerian** [claim](#stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles): Decompose the even-degree remainder of single edges into cycles -> Cycle-only partition of Eulerian graphs

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-prefix-charge**: For a nonnegative, finitely supported, not identically zero sequence, there is an index with positive term whose prefix sum through a window of K further terms is bounded by P_0 times that term.
- **inv-linear-partition**: Every finite simple undirected graph has an edge partition into linearly many cycles and single edges.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-empty-sequence**: Apply the prefix selection to an all-zero sequence. -> No index with a positive term exists, so no valid index can be returned.
- **anti-directed-graphs**: Use the partition theorem to certify schedules on directed graphs. -> The statement concerns undirected graphs; orientation is ignored.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The proof of the prefix-selection lemma is an explicit finite procedure that moves along the sequence to a window maximum until the inequality holds; the graph decomposition theorem itself is existential and is not encoded.

```python
# INPUTS: line 1 integer K >= 0; line 2 space-separated nonnegative integers S_0..S_n
# PRECONDITIONS: at least one S_j is positive; P0 = 4*(K+1)
# OUTPUTS: one index i with S_i > 0 and sum of S_0..S_{i+K} at most P0*S_i
# TIME: polynomial in the sequence length and K
# SPACE: linear in the sequence length
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers
# FAILURE: invalid input (all zero) raises an exception and exits nonzero
import sys


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split('\n')
    K = int(data[0].strip())
    S = [int(x) for x in data[1].split()]
    P0 = 4*(K+1)
    i = next(j for j, s in enumerate(S) if s > 0)
    while sum(S[:i+K+1]) > P0*S[i]:
        w = S[i+1:i+K+1]
        M = max(w)
        i = i + 1 + w.index(M)
    print(i)


main()
```

    "oracle_code": "import sys\n\nlines = open(sys.argv[1]).read().split('\\n')\nK = int(lines[0].strip())\nS = [int(x) for x in lines[1].split()]\nout = open(sys.argv[2]).read().split()\nif len(out) != 1 or not out[0].isdigit():\n    sys.exit(1)\ni = int(out[0])\nvalid = False\nif 0 <= i < len(S) and S[i] > 0:\n    total = 0\n    for j in range(min(len(S), i + K + 1)):\n        total += S[j]\n    valid = total <= 4 * (K + 1) * S[i]\nsys.exit(0 if (valid and sys.argv[3] == 'valid') else 1)\n",
    "output_contract": "stdout: a single integer index.",
    "reason": "The proof of the prefix-selection lemma is an explicit finite procedure that moves along the sequence to a window maximum until the inequality holds; the graph decomposition theorem itself is existential and is not encoded.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: line 1 integer K >= 0; line 2 space-separated nonnegative integers S_0..S_n\n# PRECONDITIONS: at least one S_j is positive; P0 = 4*(K+1)\n# OUTPUTS: one index i with S_i > 0 and sum of S_0..S_{i+K} at most P0*S_i\n# TIME: polynomial in the sequence length and K\n# SPACE: linear in the sequence length\n# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers\n# FAILURE: invalid input (all zero) raises an exception and exits nonzero\nimport sys\n\n\ndef main():\n    data = sys.stdin.read().split('\\n')\n    K = int(data[0].strip())\n    S = [int(x) for x in data[1].split()]\n    P0 = 4*(K+1)\n    i = next(j for j, s in enumerate(S) if s > 0)\n    while sum(S[:i+K+1]) > P0*S[i]:\n        w = S[i+1:i+K+1]\n        M = max(w)\n        i = i + 1 + w.index(M)\n    print(i)\n\n\nmain()\n",
    "test_vectors": [
      {
        "expected": "valid",
        "input": "1\n5\n"
      },

        "expected": "valid",
        "input": "2\n1 0 0 0 0\n"
      },

      },

      }

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an existence bound for edge partitions through an induction that mixes probabilistic choices and parameters tied to an enormous scale cutoff. It gives no specified procedure that could be shipped as a library routine. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the result concerns simulation, game states or dynamics. It is a static statement about edge partitions of abstract simple graphs. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only constructive fragment inside the paper is the endpoint-controlled path partition, derived from Lovász's theorem. A graph-analysis tool would still need the full argument, which is existential in its probabilistic steps. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The inductive proof selects random assignments and prefixes by existence arguments. No algorithm with a running time is stated, and the constant C is not computed. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no representation, index or query structure. Its boxes, pieces and quotient graphs are proof devices only. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No parallel or circuit-level structure is analysed. The vertex-folding and routing steps carry no hardware cost model. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, adversary or security notion appears. The result neither builds nor breaks a primitive. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no learning problem, data distribution or generalisation claim. Its probabilistic lemmas concern random edge and vertex sampling inside a proof. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem bounds the minimum number of parts but gives no method to find an optimal or near-optimal partition. It cannot serve as a solver or a certified optimum. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Graphs here are abstract and nothing models messages, failures or consensus. The hypergraph matching theorem is used only to pick disjoint routes inside a proof. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main value is mathematical: it settles a long-standing conjecture and supplies reusable expansion and routing lemmas. No separate engineering domain is supported by the source. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-undirected-simple-graphs** (hypothesis): Every result is stated for finite, simple, undirected graphs. Directed graphs, multigraphs and infinite graphs are outside the proved scope. Blocked use: ['Certifying cycle schedules on directed graphs', 'Applying the partition bound to multigraphs without first removing loops and parallel edges']
- **limit-cycle-length-convention** (formal_scope): A cycle is simple and has length at least three. Two-edge closed walks do not count as parts of a partition. Blocked use: ['Counting a doubled edge as a cycle part', 'Using the bound for a notion of cycle that allows repeated vertices']
- **limit-absolute-constant-unspecified** (nonconstructive): The main theorem asserts that an absolute constant exists. The paper's final choice of the constant depends on a lower scale cutoff and on parameters chosen earlier in the induction, and no explicit numerical value is given. Blocked use: ['Quoting a concrete number of parts for a given graph', 'Using the bound as a hard runtime or memory budget in an implementation']
- **limit-small-graphs-single-edges** (edge_case): The linear bound is only asserted up to the unspecified constant. For small graphs the proof simply takes every edge singly, so the bound says nothing sharp there. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem to claim few parts for graphs of small order', 'Reading the bound as a tight count of parts for forests or sparse graphs']
- **limit-sufficiently-large-scale** (hypothesis): The small-residue lemma holds only for all sufficiently large scale D, after the fixed positive parameters eta and c are chosen. Its thresholds depend on those parameters. Blocked use: ['Applying the 9r completed-parts bound at small scale', 'Choosing eta or c after the threshold has been fixed']
- **limit-routing-load-and-expansion** (hypothesis): Team-constrained routing needs a bounded number of demands per vertex together with a cut expansion that is large compared with path length, team size and forbidden-set size. Without these the routes need not exist. Blocked use: ['Routing demands in graphs of weak expansion', 'Allowing unbounded endpoint load at a vertex']
- **limit-pair-folding-degree-conditions** (hypothesis): Pair folding needs disjoint folding sets with bounded degree and bounded neighbour counts in each set. A degree bound at the paired vertices alone is not enough. Blocked use: ['Folding vertices of a complete bipartite side without a neighbour bound', 'Treating the error-edge bound as valid for overlapping folding sets']
- **limit-edge-split-probabilistic-existence** (nonconstructive): The edge-splitting lemma is proved by a random assignment that succeeds with positive probability. It gives existence of the split, not a deterministic procedure. Blocked use: ['Presenting the lemma as a deterministic partition routine with a stated running time']
- **limit-prefix-nonzero-sequence** (edge_case): Prefix selection needs a sequence that is not identically zero. For an all-zero sequence there is no index with a positive term, so the lemma returns nothing. Blocked use: ['Calling the selection on an all-zero sequence', 'Using the lemma for sequences with infinite support']

Scope note of family 181 (`lean/docs/181.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A linear cycle-and-edge decomposition of every graph](../../preprints/A-linear-cycle-and-edge-decomposition-of-every-graph-September-24-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Erdős–Gallai cycle-decomposition conjecture asks for a linear bound on the number of cycles and single edges needed to partition a graph's edges. The formalized result gives one absolute constant $C>0$ such that every finite simple graph on $n$ vertices has an edge-disjoint decomposition into at most $Cn$ cycles or singleton edges. Edgeless and small graphs are included. The optimal value of $C$ is not determined.

Comparator source `CycleDecomposition.lean` (850 bytes, sha256 `8c40255789147b218565654daa7d4f9ef39824b009d06ec9fb6beb7b45cae0f9`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section


open Filter Asymptotics Real
open scoped Topology

open MeasureTheory ProbabilityTheory Finset

namespace ErdosGallai

def CycleOrSingleEdge {V : Type} (G : SimpleGraph V)
    (s : Set (Sym2 V)) : Prop :=
  (∃ (v : V) (p : G.Walk v v), p.IsCycle ∧ s = p.edgeSet) ∨
  ∃ e ∈ G.edgeSet, s = {e}

def EdgeDecomposition {V : Type} (G : SimpleGraph V) (k : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ parts : Fin k → Set (Sym2 V),
    (∀ i, CycleOrSingleEdge G (parts i)) ∧
    Pairwise (fun i j => Disjoint (parts i) (parts j)) ∧
    (⋃ i, parts i) = G.edgeSet


def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ, 0 < C ∧ ∀ (n : ℕ) (G : SimpleGraph (Fin n)),
    ∃ k : ℕ, EdgeDecomposition G k ∧ (k : ℝ) ≤ C * n

theorem erdos_gallai : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end ErdosGallai

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every finite simple undirected graph on <i>n</i> vertices has an edge partition into at most $`Cn`$ simple cycles and single edges, for an absolute constant <i>C</i>. This resolves the Erdős–Gallai cycle decomposition conjecture positively.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

How efficiently can the edges of a graph be partitioned into cycles? Single edges must also be allowed: a forest contains no cycle. The Erdős–Gallai cycle decomposition conjecture asserts that this necessary allowance suffices for a universal linear bound. Here and throughout, a cycle is simple and has length at least three.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is an absolute constant $C>0$ such that, for every finite simple undirected graph $G$ with $n$ vertices, $E(G)$ has a partition into at most $Cn$ parts, each of which is the edge set of a cycle or consists of a single edge.*

</div>

The parts in this theorem may share vertices. They share no edges, and every edge of $G$ occurs in exactly one part. Isolated vertices need not be covered, and the empty partition is permitted when $E(G)=\varnothing$.

The conjecture appears in the 1966 paper of Erdős, Goodman and Pósa (Erdős, Goodman, and Pósa 1966, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[EGP1966](#ref-EGP1966)], which explicitly distinguishes unrestricted covers from edge-disjoint covers and records an $O(n\log n)$ upper bound. Conlon, Fox and Sudakov (Conlon, Fox, and Sudakov 2014) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[CFS2014](#ref-CFS2014)] improved the general bound to $O(n\log\log n)$ in 2014. Bucić and Montgomery (Bucić and Montgomery 2024) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)] subsequently proved the bound $O(n\log^*n)$; their paper appeared in journal form in 2024. Here $\log^*n$ is the number of logarithm iterations needed to reach at most one. [thm:main](#thm:main) establishes the conjectured linear bound. Its order is optimal, since a tree on $n$ vertices requires $n-1$ single-edge parts. More sharply, complete bipartite examples require $(3/2-o(1))n$ parts; see (Bucić and Montgomery 2024, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)] for this lower bound and its history.

Linear bounds were already known for important classes. Conlon, Fox and Sudakov (Conlon, Fox, and Sudakov 2014, Theorems 1.3 and 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CFS2014](#ref-CFS2014)] proved them with high probability for binomial random graphs, and deterministically for graphs of linear minimum degree. Korándi, Krivelevich and Sudakov (Korándi, Krivelevich, and Sudakov 2015) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KKS2015](#ref-KKS2015)] obtained asymptotically sharp counts over a broad range of random-graph probabilities. For every fixed $\alpha,\delta>0$, Girão, Granet, Kühn and Osthus (Girão et al. 2021, Theorem 1.10(iii)) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GGKO2021](#ref-GGKO2021)] proved that sufficiently large graphs of minimum degree at least $\alpha n$ admit at most $(3/2+\delta)n$ cycles and edges. Akbari, Aloni, Beikmohammadi and Clow (Akbari et al. 2025, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[AABC2025](#ref-AABC2025)] proved the bound $n-1$ for graphs of positive order and maximum degree at most four. The general problem allows both sparse regions and vertices of widely differing degrees.

The edge-disjoint requirement is essential to the problem. If cycles and edges may overlap, Pyber (Pyber 1985) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Pyber1985](#ref-Pyber1985)] proved that $n-1$ parts suffice to cover every graph of positive order. Akbari, Aloni, Beikmohammadi and Clow (Akbari et al. 2025, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[AABC2025](#ref-AABC2025)] improved this covering bound to $n-2$ for graphs containing a cycle. A cover of that kind does not supply a partition: deleting repeated edges may break its cycles into many paths. Our construction keeps a separate assignment for every original edge throughout.

also yields two cycle-only consequences. Here a graph is *Eulerian* if all its degrees are even; connectivity is not required.

<a id="stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles"></a>
<div id="cor:eulerian-cycles" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *Every finite simple undirected Eulerian graph on $n$ vertices has an edge partition into at most $Cn$ cycles, with $C$ from [thm:main](#thm:main).*

*For every fixed $\delta,p>0$, there exist $\varepsilon>0$ and an integer $n_0\ge1$ such that every finite simple undirected Eulerian graph $G$ on $n\ge n_0$ vertices has an edge partition into at most $\Delta(G)/2+\delta n$ cycles, provided that every partition $V(G)=A\mathbin{\dot\cup}B$ with $|A|,|B|\ge\varepsilon n$ satisfies $$e_G(A,B)\ge p|A||B|.$$ Here $\Delta(G)$ denotes the maximum degree, and $e_G(A,B)$ is the number of edges joining $A$ to $B$.*

</div>

Any edge partition of $G$ into cycles needs at least $\Delta(G)/2$ cycles, since a cycle uses at most two edges incident with a given vertex. The second assertion attains this lower bound up to an arbitrarily small linear error under the stated large-cut condition.

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-eulerian-cycles</summary>

*Proof.* For the first claim, let the partition from [thm:main](#thm:main) have $r$ cycle parts and $s$ single-edge parts, with $r+s\le Cn$. The graph formed by the latter edges has even degrees, since deleting cycles preserves degree parity. Repeatedly removing a cycle preserves this property and ends with no edges: every even graph with an edge contains a cycle. This uses at most $s$ cycles, for a total of at most $r+s\le Cn$. The equivalence of this $O(n)$ Eulerian cycle bound with the Erdős–Gallai conjecture is recorded in (Girão et al. 2021, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GGKO2021](#ref-GGKO2021)].

The partition-cut condition in the second claim is exactly the weakly-$(\varepsilon,p)$-quasirandom condition of (Girão et al. 2021, sec. 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GGKO2021](#ref-GGKO2021)]. Their Proposition 6.3 proves that Conjecture 1.14, the stated bound with these quantifiers, is equivalent to their Erdős–Gallai Conjecture 1.4, supplied by [thm:main](#thm:main). ◻

</details>

Our argument builds on the expansion, hypergraph matching and path-closing methods of Bucić and Montgomery (Bucić and Montgomery 2024) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)], with earlier reservoir and sparse-cut methods of Conlon, Fox and Sudakov (Conlon, Fox, and Sudakov 2014) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[CFS2014](#ref-CFS2014)]. We use Lovász’s path-and-cycle decomposition theorem (Lovász 1968) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Lovasz1968](#ref-Lovasz1968)] and the Aharoni–Haxell matching theorem (Aharoni and Haxell 2000) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[AH2000](#ref-AH2000)], in the precise forms stated below. The additional mechanism is an induction that saves vertices across different scales while reserving enough edges to lift cycles from the smaller graphs. A decomposition that repeatedly pays a cost proportional to the full graph order can accumulate a factor from the number of scales. Here the work over a suitable prefix is charged to one expanding layer, and pair identifications save a fixed fraction of that layer’s order. The savings pay for the work outside the recursive calls.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Outline of the proof

All logarithms below are to base two. A graph has cut expansion $h$ when every cut has at least $h$ times the size of its smaller side in crossing edges. We split the graph at successively smaller scales $D_j=n^{(0.95)^j}$. At scale $D$, a small collection of long cycles is removed, and the remaining edges are assigned to boxes of order at most $D^{1.02}$. Their vertex sets may overlap, but the total order increases by only a factor $1+O(1/\log D)$. Within each box, we extract vertex-disjoint pieces with cut expansion $D^{0.90}$. Each residual box graph has average degree at most $D^{0.95}$, so the construction continues at the next scale.

Processing every expanding piece at every scale would accumulate too much cost. Instead, we choose a layer whose total piece order pays for an entire prefix, including a fixed number of further scales. The pieces of this layer have two roles. First, an expanding graph can be partitioned into linearly many completed parts and residual graphs whose total order is an arbitrarily small fraction of its own order (). Second, part of its edge set can be reserved for routing without destroying that conclusion. The sparse graphs passed to the next scale retain their full box vertex sets; the small-residue graphs produced when pieces are processed are eventually handled by induction.

We describe the nonterminal case; the terminal cases are treated separately in [sec:induction](#sec:induction). After the fixed gap in scales, the unprocessed edges are sparse and live on very small descendant boxes. We gather those boxes into batches. Within each earlier box $Y$, all but $O(\left|Y\right|/\log D)$ vertices have small degree in the union of that box’s batch graphs. We pair these vertices in sufficiently large intersections between a batch and a selected expanding piece, and identify each pair. Edges between a folding set and vertices having too many neighbors in that set are first partitioned into paths; reserved routes close the trimmed remnants into cycles at linear cost. The remaining graph can then be simplified after pair identification by setting aside only linearly many edges as single-edge parts ().

The quotient graphs have fewer vertices, so induction partitions their edges into cycles and single edges. Each quotient edge retains one original representative. At a paired vertex, the representatives of a quotient cycle may enter through one member of the pair and leave through the other. We join these two members by a path in the reserved expanding piece. The routing lemma chooses these paths edge-disjointly, with interiors that avoid the representative endpoints and are pairwise disjoint within each cycle (). Consequently each quotient cycle lifts to one simple cycle. The degree bound is imposed on the union of the batch graphs within each earlier box, so the routing load does not grow with the number of batches.

In this case, write $S$ for the total order of the selected layer. All work outside the recursive calls costs $O(S+n/\log D)$ parts. The quotient orders sum to at most $n-S/3+O(n/\log D)$, and the small-residue graphs have total order at most $S/100$. In the resulting cost estimate, choosing $C$ sufficiently large makes the coefficient of $S$ nonpositive. To absorb the remaining $O(Cn/\log D)$ overhead, we prove the stronger inductive bound $C\phi(n)n$, where $$\phi(n)=\max\left\{\frac12,\,
                 1-\frac1{\sqrt{\log n}}\right\}
 \quad(n>1),\qquad \phi(1)=\frac12.$$ The quotient graphs have order at most $D^{0.30}$. Their smaller value of $\phi$ supplies a gain of order $1/\sqrt{\log D}$, which dominates the $O(1/\log D)$ overlap cost. All recursive graphs are strictly smaller, and all scale requirements are fixed before the final constant $C$ is chosen.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes the graph conventions and the two external theorem inputs. Sections <a href="#sec:routing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:routing">3</a> and <a href="#sec:residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:residue">4</a> establish the routing and small-residue tools. Section <a href="#sec:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:splitting">5</a> constructs the scale layers, Section <a href="#sec:folding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:folding">6</a> combines folding with those tools, and Section <a href="#sec:induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:induction">7</a> completes the induction.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Conventions and preliminary results

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Graphs, partitions and scales

Unless explicitly stated otherwise, graphs are finite, simple and undirected. A path is simple and has at least one edge. The *order* of a graph is its number of vertices, including any isolated vertices that have been retained. Temporary quotients formed by identifying vertices may have loops or parallel edges; these are removed before any decomposition theorem is applied.

An indexed family of subgraphs partitions a graph when their assigned edge sets are pairwise disjoint and have the required union. Each subgraph carries only its assigned edges, rather than all edges induced by its vertex set. Different indices may have overlapping, or even equal, vertex sets. Order sums count those indices separately. Whenever edges from different members are combined, we specify the assignment and account for the edges that remain.

For disjoint vertex sets $A,B$, write $E_H(A,B)$ for the set of edges of $H$ between them and $e_H(A,B)=|E_H(A,B)|$. Write $\partial_H U$ for the edge boundary of $U\subseteq V(H)$. An $r$-vertex graph $H$ has *cut expansion at least $h$* if <a id="eq:cut-expansion"></a>$$\label{eq:cut-expansion}
 |\partial_H U|\ge h\min\{|U|,r-|U|\}
 \qquad\text{for every }U\subseteq V(H).$$ If $r\ge2$, singleton cuts show that its minimum degree is at least $h$, and consequently $r\ge h+1$.

All logarithms denoted by $\log$ are to base two; $\ln$ denotes the natural logarithm. Scales $D$ are real, and decimal exponents denote exact rational numbers. Inequalities comparing integer orders with real bounds have their literal meaning. Asymptotic notation always refers to the scale tending to infinity. Once the explicitly fixed parameters of a lemma have been chosen, its implicit constants and lower scale threshold are uniform over the graphs and orders allowed by that lemma. In the final induction the finite list of parameters is fixed first, a common lower cutoff $D_*$ is fixed next, and only then is $C$ chosen.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### A path decomposition with controlled endpoints

We use the following form of Lovász’s decomposition theorem. Disjointness in its original statement means edge-disjointness (Lovász 1968, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Lovasz1968](#ref-Lovasz1968)]; see also (Bucić and Montgomery 2024, Theorem 21) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)].

<a id="stmt-thm-lovasz"></a>
<div id="thm:lovasz" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Lovász). *The edges of a finite simple graph on $n$ vertices can be partitioned into at most $\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor$ paths and cycles.*

</div>

The following consequence is recorded in (Bucić and Montgomery 2024, Corollary 22) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)]. We include the deduction because endpoint multiplicities are used in two different constructions.

<a id="stmt-cor-endpoint-paths"></a>
<div id="cor:endpoint-paths" class="corollary">

**Corollary 4**. *Every finite simple graph on $n$ vertices has an edge partition into paths such that each vertex is an endpoint of at most two paths. In particular, the partition has at most $n$ paths.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-endpoint-paths"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-endpoint-paths</summary>

*Proof.* For $n=0$ there is nothing to prove. Otherwise add a vertex $v_0$ and join it to precisely the even-degree vertices of the original graph, including the isolated vertices. Every old vertex now has odd degree. In a path-and-cycle partition its number of path-endpoint occurrences must therefore be odd and positive.

Apply [thm:lovasz](#thm:lovasz) to the augmented graph, and let $p$ be the number of path parts. The old vertices force $2p\ge n$, whereas the theorem gives $$\left\lceil n/2\right\rceil\le p
 \le \text{number of parts}
 \le \left\lfloor(n+1)/2\right\rfloor=\left\lceil n/2\right\rceil.$$ Thus every part is a path. Each old vertex is an endpoint exactly once: an old vertex with at least three endpoint occurrences, together with the other old vertices, would force at least $n+2$ occurrences, although $2p\le n+1$.

Delete $v_0$ from these paths and discard any edgeless remnants. Every old vertex is incident to at most one deleted edge, so it gains at most one new endpoint occurrence. The remaining paths partition the original edge set and have endpoint load at most two. Each positive-length path contributes two endpoint occurrences, proving the final count. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Disjoint representatives for hypergraphs

A hypergraph here is a finite family of nonempty subsets of a finite ground set. A matching is a subfamily of pairwise disjoint edges. We use the following sufficient condition, from the hypergraph Hall theorem of Aharoni and Haxell (Aharoni and Haxell 2000) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[AH2000](#ref-AH2000)], in the bounded-rank form stated in (Bucić and Montgomery 2024, Theorem 6) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)].

<a id="stmt-thm-aharoni-haxell"></a>
<div id="thm:aharoni-haxell" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Aharoni–Haxell). *Let $s$ be a positive integer and let $(\mathcal H_i)_{i\in J}$ be a finite indexed family of hypergraphs on a common ground set. Suppose every edge is nonempty and has size at most $s$. If, for every nonempty $I\subseteq J$, the union $\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal H_i$ has a matching of size strictly greater than $s(|I|-1)$, then there are edges $E_i\in\mathcal H_i$, one for each $i\in J$, that are pairwise disjoint.*

</div>

One may pass from the uniform-size version to this statement by padding each edge occurrence with private fresh vertices. Every matching before padding remains a matching after padding, and disjoint representatives after padding have disjoint original projections. The hypergraphs need not be distinct, and equal edges in different indexed hypergraphs cause no difficulty.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Elementary probabilistic estimates

We use linearity of expectation, Markov’s inequality, the union bound, and the following binomial estimates. They are stated explicitly to fix constants for the sampling arguments.

<a id="stmt-lem-chernoff"></a>
<div id="lem:chernoff" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *If $X$ is binomial with mean $\mu$, then $$\Pr(X\le\mu/2)\le e^{-\mu/8},
 \qquad
 \Pr(X\ge2\mu)\le e^{-\mu/3}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-chernoff"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-chernoff</summary>

*Proof.* For $X$ a sum of independent Bernoulli variables, $\mathbb Ee^{tX}\le\exp(\mu(e^t-1))$. Apply Markov’s inequality with $t=-\ln2$ for the lower tail and $t=\ln2$ for the upper tail. The resulting exponents are $-\mu(1-\ln2)/2$ and $-\mu(2\ln2-1)$, respectively. They are at most $-\mu/8$ and $-\mu/3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:routing"></a>
### Routing with team constraints

A demand specifies two distinct vertices to be joined. Demands are indexed, so several demands may specify the same pair. Besides edge-disjointness of all routes, we will need internal vertex-disjointness for specified groups of routes. The following lemma supplies both requirements. Its proof uses the hypergraph formulation and expansion-to-connectivity method of Bucić and Montgomery (Bucić and Montgomery 2024, Proposition 8 and Lemma 9) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[BM2024](#ref-BM2024)]. The vertex tokens introduced in the proof impose a separate internal-disjointness constraint for each team; different teams compete only for graph edges.

<a id="stmt-lem-routing"></a>
<div id="lem:routing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:routing-condition"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Routing). *Let $P$ be a graph of order $r\ge 2$ with cut expansion $h>0$, and put <a id="eq:routing-radii"></a>$$\label{eq:routing-radii}
  g=\left\lceil 8(r/h)\log(2r)\right\rceil,\qquad
  \ell=2g\bigl(1+\left\lceil\log r\right\rceil\bigr).$$ Let $\mathcal D$ be a finite set of demands, where demand $p$ has distinct endvertices $x_p,y_p\in V(P)$. Suppose that each vertex occurs as an endvertex in at most $k$ demands, where $k\ge 1$. Partition $\mathcal D$ into teams of size at most $b$, where $b\ge 1$, and give each demand a set $Z_p\subseteq V(P)$ of at most $z$ vertices, where $z\ge 0$. If $$\label{eq:routing-condition}
  h\ge 64\bigl(\ell^2(k+b)+z\bigr),$$ then there are simple $x_p$–$y_p$ paths $Q_p$ in $P$, one for each $p\in\mathcal D$, with the following properties:*

1.  *each $Q_p$ has length at most $\ell$ and no internal vertex in $Z_p$;*

2.  *the paths $Q_p$ are pairwise edge-disjoint;*

3.  *the internal vertex sets of paths in the same team are pairwise disjoint.*

*The sets $Z_p$ may contain $x_p$ or $y_p$: their restrictions concern only internal vertices. If a route must also avoid another demand’s endvertices internally, those vertices may be included in its set $Z_p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-routing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-routing</summary>

*Proof.* There is nothing to prove when $\mathcal D$ is empty. Suppose it is nonempty, and write $c(p)$ for the team of demand $p$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### A hypergraph obstruction.

Take a resource set that is the tagged disjoint union of $E(P)$ and the tokens $(v,c)$, where $v\in V(P)$ and $c$ is a team. For each demand $p$, form a hypergraph $\mathcal H_p$ on this resource set. Its hyperedges correspond to all simple $x_p$–$y_p$ paths of length at most $\ell$ whose internal vertices avoid $Z_p$. A candidate path contributes its graph edges and the token $(v,c(p))$ for each of its internal vertices. A path of length $t\ge 1$ therefore contributes $2t-1\le 2\ell$ resources. In particular all hyperedges are nonempty, and all the hypergraphs are finite. A choice of pairwise disjoint representatives from the $\mathcal H_p$ gives exactly the three required properties.

<a id="eq:routing-obstruction-budgets"></a>
We verify the sufficient condition of [thm:aharoni-haxell](#thm:aharoni-haxell) with $s=2\ell$. Suppose, for a contradiction, that it fails for a nonempty $I\subseteq\mathcal D$. Let $\mathcal M$ be a maximum matching in $\bigcup_{p\in I}\mathcal H_p$. Then $\left|\mathcal M\right|\le 2\ell(\left|I\right|-1)$. Let $F\subseteq E(P)$ be the graph-edge resources used by $\mathcal M$, and put $$Y_c=\{v\in V(P):(v,c)\text{ is used by }\mathcal M\}.$$ Each matching member uses at most $\ell$ graph edges and at most $\ell$ tokens, so $$\label{eq:routing-obstruction-budgets}
  \left|F\right|\le 2\ell^2\left|I\right|,\qquad
  \sum_c\left|Y_c\right|\le 2\ell^2\left|I\right|.$$ These estimates allow several matching members to come from one demand; no restriction on their demand indices is being imposed.

<a id="eq:routing-pair-count"></a>
<a id="eq:routing-endpoint-count"></a>
For each team let $m_c=\left|\{p\in I:c(p)=c\}\right|\le b$. Thus $$\sum_{p\in I}\left|Y_{c(p)}\right|
  =\sum_c m_c\left|Y_c\right|
  \le 2b\ell^2\left|I\right|.$$ Consequently the set $I_1=\{p\in I:\left|Y_{c(p)}\right|\le 4b\ell^2\}$ has size at least $\left|I\right|/2$. Choose an inclusion-maximal set $I_2\subseteq I_1$ whose endpoint pairs are pairwise vertex-disjoint. Every demand in $I_1$ meets one of these selected pairs. The two vertices of any selected pair occur in at most $2k$ demands in total, so $$\label{eq:routing-pair-count}
  \left|I_2\right|\ge\frac{\left|I_1\right|}{2k}
  \ge\frac{\left|I\right|}{4k}.$$ We will also use the global endpoint count $$\label{eq:routing-endpoint-count}
  2\left|I\right|\le kr.$$

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### A small ball for each selected demand.

For $p\in I_2$, define $$W_p=(Z_p\cup Y_{c(p)})\setminus\{x_p,y_p\},\qquad
  P_p=(P-F)-W_p.$$ Here the first deletion removes edges and the second removes vertices. Both endpoints remain in $P_p$. By <a href="#eq:routing-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-condition">[eq:routing-condition]</a>, <a id="eq:routing-forbidden-budget"></a>$$\label{eq:routing-forbidden-budget}
  \left|W_p\right|\le z+4b\ell^2
  \le 4\bigl(z+(k+b)\ell^2\bigr)
  \le h/16\le h/4.$$ Set $T=\ell/2=g(1+\left\lceil\log r\right\rceil)$, an integer. If the balls of radius $T$ about both $x_p$ and $y_p$ in $P_p$ had size greater than $r/2$, they would intersect. Joining their paths to an intersection vertex gives an $x_p$–$y_p$ walk of length at most $2T=\ell$; deleting closed subwalks gives a simple path of no greater length. It uses no edge of $F$ and no vertex of $W_p$. Its internal vertices are also different from $x_p,y_p$, and hence avoid both $Z_p$ and $Y_{c(p)}$. Its hyperedge in $\mathcal H_p$ is therefore disjoint from $\mathcal M$, contrary to maximality of that matching.

<a id="eq:routing-small-root-balls"></a>
For every $p\in I_2$ we may consequently choose an endpoint $s_p$ whose radius-$T$ ball in $P_p$ has size at most $r/2$. Let $S=\{s_p:p\in I_2\}$. These roots are distinct because the selected endpoint pairs are disjoint. Each root retains its demand label: write $p(s)$ for the unique $p\in I_2$ with $s=s_p$. In particular, $$\label{eq:routing-small-root-balls}
  \left|\{v:\operatorname{dist}_{P_{p(s)}}(s,v)\le T\}\right|\le r/2
  \qquad(s\in S).$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### Growth with different forbidden sets.

<a id="eq:routing-union-growth"></a>
<a id="eq:routing-growth-threshold"></a>
The deleted-edge budget may be too large to grow a ball from one root. We first grow unions of balls, retaining each root’s graph, and then alternate halving the root set with further growth until one root remains. For a nonempty $A\subseteq S$ and an integer $t\ge 0$, put $$B_t(A)=\bigcup_{s\in A}
  \{v\in V(P_{p(s)}):\operatorname{dist}_{P_{p(s)}}(s,v)\le t\}.$$ These are unions of balls in their respective graphs; the forbidden set is kept with its root throughout. We claim that $$\label{eq:routing-union-growth}
  \left|B_{t+1}(A)\right|
  \ge\left(1+\frac{h}{2r}\right)\left|B_t(A)\right|$$ whenever $$\label{eq:routing-growth-threshold}
  0<\left|B_t(A)\right|\le r/2,
  \qquad \left|F\right|\le h\left|B_t(A)\right|/4.$$

To prove this, let $B=B_t(A)$ and assign to each $v\in B$ one root $s(v)\in A$ that reaches $v$ within $t$ steps in $P_{p(s(v))}$. Consider a boundary edge $vw$ of $B$, with $v\in B$ and $w\notin B$. It extends this particular root’s path whenever $vw\notin F$ and $w\notin W_{p(s(v))}$. In that event $w\in B_{t+1}(A)\setminus B$. Because $P$ is simple, at most $\left|W_{p(s(v))}\right|\le h/4$ boundary edges at $v$ fail the latter condition. Cut expansion and <a href="#eq:routing-growth-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-growth-threshold">[eq:routing-growth-threshold]</a> therefore leave at least $$h\left|B\right|-(h/4)\left|B\right|-\left|F\right|\ge(h/2)\left|B\right|$$ usable boundary edges. Each new vertex receives at most $r$ such edges. Thus at least $h\left|B\right|/(2r)$ new vertices are reached, proving <a href="#eq:routing-union-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-union-growth">[eq:routing-union-growth]</a>.

<a id="eq:routing-growth-block"></a>
It follows that, for every integer $t\ge 0$, $$\label{eq:routing-growth-block}
  \left|B_t(A)\right|\ge 1\ \text{ and }\quad
  \left|F\right|\le h\left|B_t(A)\right|/4
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \left|B_{t+g}(A)\right|>r/2.$$ Indeed, the conclusion is immediate by monotonicity if the starting union already has size greater than $r/2$. Otherwise, if it still had size at most $r/2$ after $g$ more steps, all intervening unions would have size at most $r/2$, while their monotonicity would preserve the edge-deletion threshold. We could apply <a href="#eq:routing-union-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-union-growth">[eq:routing-union-growth]</a> at all $g$ steps. Singleton cuts in the simple graph $P$ give $0<h\le r-1<r$, and the elementary inequality $\log(1+h/(2r))\ge h/(4r)$ then yields $$\left|B_{t+g}(A)\right|
  \ge \left(1+\frac{h}{2r}\right)^g\left|B_t(A)\right|
  \ge 2^{gh/(4r)}
  \ge (2r)^2>r/2,$$ a contradiction. This also verifies the growth time with the ceiling in the definition of $g$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Restarting after halving the roots.

Initially $B_0(S)=S$. By <a href="#eq:routing-obstruction-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-obstruction-budgets">[eq:routing-obstruction-budgets]</a>, <a href="#eq:routing-pair-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-pair-count">[eq:routing-pair-count]</a>, and <a href="#eq:routing-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-condition">[eq:routing-condition]</a>, $$\left|F\right|\le 2\ell^2\left|I\right|
  \le 8k\ell^2\left|S\right|\le h\left|S\right|/4.$$ The growth block <a href="#eq:routing-growth-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-growth-block">[eq:routing-growth-block]</a> gives $\left|B_g(S)\right|>r/2$.

Suppose now that a nonempty root set $A$ has $\left|B_t(A)\right|>r/2$ and $\left|A\right|\ge 2$. Partition $A$ into two nonempty sets whose sizes differ by at most one. Their radius-$t$ ball unions together cover $B_t(A)$, so one of them, say $A'$, satisfies $$\left|A'\right|\le\left\lceil\left|A\right|/2\right\rceil,\qquad
  \left|B_t(A')\right|>r/4.$$ The original matching has a bound independent of the number of retained roots: by <a href="#eq:routing-obstruction-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-obstruction-budgets">[eq:routing-obstruction-budgets]</a>, <a href="#eq:routing-endpoint-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-endpoint-count">[eq:routing-endpoint-count]</a>, and <a href="#eq:routing-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-condition">[eq:routing-condition]</a>, <a id="eq:routing-restart-budget"></a>$$\label{eq:routing-restart-budget}
  \left|F\right|\le 2\ell^2\left|I\right|
  \le \ell^2kr\le hr/64
  < h\left|B_t(A')\right|/4.$$ Thus another growth block gives $\left|B_{t+g}(A')\right|>r/2$.

Start with $A=S$ at radius $g$ and repeat this operation until only one root remains. After $j$ halvings the number of roots is at most $\left\lceil\left|S\right|/2^j\right\rceil$; this follows inductively from rounding each half upward. Hence at most $\left\lceil\log\left|S\right|\right\rceil$ halvings are needed, including zero if $\left|S\right|=1$. At termination a single root has a ball larger than $r/2$ at an integer radius at most $$g\bigl(1+\left\lceil\log\left|S\right|\right\rceil\bigr)
  \le g\bigl(1+\left\lceil\log r\right\rceil\bigr)=T.$$ This contradicts <a href="#eq:routing-small-root-balls" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:routing-small-root-balls">[eq:routing-small-root-balls]</a> and monotonicity of that root’s balls.

There is therefore no set $I$ for which the matching condition fails. Applying [thm:aharoni-haxell](#thm:aharoni-haxell) gives disjoint hypergraph representatives, and their paths prove the lemma. ◻

</details>

For later estimates, the exact radii also give the useful uniform bound <a id="eq:routing-length-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:routing-length-bound}
  \ell\le 108(r/h)(\log r)^2
  =O\bigl((r/h)(\log r)^2\bigr).$$ Indeed, $h<r$ and $r\ge 2$ imply $g\le 9(r/h)\log(2r)$, $\log(2r)\le 2\log r$, and $1+\left\lceil\log r\right\rceil\le 3\log r$.

<a id="sec:residue"></a>
### Expansion in small reservoirs

We next process a graph containing a spanning expanding subgraph. Reserving vertices and edges to connect and close paths is already central to the cycle decomposition argument of Conlon, Fox and Sudakov (Conlon, Fox, and Sudakov 2014, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[CFS2014](#ref-CFS2014)]. Our aim here is to leave residual graphs with arbitrarily small total vertex order, so that their recursive cost can be paid by the later folding savings. At a scale $D$, put $$\sigma=D^{0.90},\qquad \tau=D^{0.74}.$$ Throughout this section the order $r$ lies in $\sigma\le r\le D^{1.02}$. All assertions for sufficiently large $D$ are uniform over the graphs and orders in this range. Their thresholds may depend on the fixed positive constants in the statements.
